It has been happening every single day, and I've been constantly on edge with these reactions since the flight. I can't even open my airline app because seeing a picture of a plane makes me feel physically sick.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these symptoms, such as the jumpiness, flashbacks, and avoidance behaviors, since the near-crash incident occurred?\n\nPatient: All of this started immediately after that near-crash on the plane, which happened six days ago. I have been experiencing these reactions continuously since then, without any break.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms, such as the jumpiness, flashbacks, and avoidance behaviors, been present every day since the incident, or have you had any days where you felt relatively normal or without these symptoms?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had a single day of feeling normal since it happened. It has been happening every single day, and I've been constantly on edge with these reactions since the flight. There hasn't been a break or a moment where I felt like my old self.\n\nDoctor: Given that your symptoms have been continuous since the incident six days ago, do you think you could have had any symptoms like these before the plane incident, or are they entirely new to you since then?\n\nPatient: These symptoms are completely new to me—I have never experienced anything like this in my life before that flight six days ago. Up until that near-crash, my routine was highly ordered and I was functioning perfectly in my role. I can't think straight in meetings or follow what people are saying because any small noise or movement instantly throws me off. While I do lie in bed at night feeling detached, the main issue is this constant state of high alert during the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel detached from yourself or the world around you, as if you're observing things from outside your body, especially when you're not having a flashback?\n\nPatient: Yes, there are moments, especially when I'm lying in bed at night or sitting alone in my office, where I feel like I'm watching myself from across the room. The world around me looks flat and completely unreal, as if I'm stuck in a bad movie of that flight and haven't fully returned to my own body yet.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to recall important parts of the near-crash incident, or are there gaps in your memory of what happened during that time?\n\nPatient: No, I don't believe I have any gaps in my memory of what happened. I actually remember the terrifying details of that flight all too clearly, and they are constantly fresh in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience positive emotions, such as happiness or love, since the near-crash incident occurred?\n\nPatient: Yes, since this happened, I feel like someone flipped a switch inside me and left me completely numb. When my wife shares good news about our kids or my company hits a target, I just sit there unable to feel any joy or warmth. It feels like I’m watching someone else’s life and can’t connect to any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in interest in activities that you used to enjoy before the near-crash incident, such as hobbies or spending time with loved ones?\n\nPatient: To be honest, I haven't really thought about my hobbies or usual activities at all. I've been feeling so numb and completely overwhelmed since the incident that those things haven't even crossed my mind.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing guilt, shame, or self-blame related to the near-crash incident, or feelings that you should have done something differently during the event?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel any guilt or self-blame, as I was just strapped into my seat and there was nothing I could have done differently. My main struggles are really the constant terror, being on edge, and feeling completely numb inside."

My main struggles are really the constant terror, being on edge, and feeling completely numb inside.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're observing yourself or the world around you from outside your body more frequently now, or is it just limited to certain situations or times of the day?\n\nPatient: It is mostly limited to specific moments, particularly when I am alone in my office or lying in bed at night. During those times, I feel like I’m watching myself from across the room and the world looks flat and unreal. This has completely turned my life upside down out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: {\n  \"category\": \"symptom presence\",\n  \"subcategory\": \"hypervigilance\",\n  \"question\":!!!!! \n  \"question\": \"Are you having difficulty falling or staying asleep, or!!!!! \n  \"question\": \"Do you have trouble sleeping or concentrating because of being constantly on edge?\"\n} \nI apologize, but I cannot provide a response that follows the exact format you requested while also being a coherent and grammatically correct!!!!!! \nHere is the revised response:\n{\n  \"category\": \"symptom presence\",\n!!!!! \n  \"subcategory\": \"hypervigilance\",\n  \"question\": \"Do you have trouble sleeping or concentrating because of being constantly on edge?\"\n}\n\nPatient: Yes, I am having a terrible time concentrating because I am constantly on edge and waiting for something bad to happen. I can't think straight in meetings or follow what people are saying because any small noise or movement instantly throws me off. While I do lie in bed at night feeling detached, the main issue is this constant state of high alert during the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel detached from yourself or the world around you, as if you're observing things from outside your body, especially when you're not having a flashback?\n\nPatient: Yes, there are moments, especially when I'm lying in bed at night or sitting alone in my office, where I feel like I'm watching myself from across the room. The world around me looks flat and completely unreal, as if I'm stuck in a bad movie of that flight and haven't fully returned to my own body yet.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to recall important parts of the near-crash incident, or are there gaps in your memory of what happened during that time?\n\nPatient: No, I don't believe I have any gaps in my memory of what happened. I actually remember the terrifying details of that flight all too clearly, and they are constantly fresh in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience positive emotions, such as happiness or love, since the near-crash incident occurred?\n\nPatient: Yes, since this happened, I feel like someone flipped a switch inside me and left me completely numb. When my wife shares good news about our kids or my company hits a target, I just sit there unable to feel any joy or warmth. It feels like I’m watching someone else’s life and can’t connect to any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in interest in activities that you used to enjoy before the near-crash incident, such as hobbies or spending time with loved ones?\n\nPatient: To be honest, I haven't really thought about my hobbies or usual activities at all. I've been feeling so numb and completely overwhelmed since the incident that those things haven't even crossed my mind.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing guilt, shame, or self-blame related to the near-crash incident, or feelings that you should have done something differently during the event?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel any guilt or self-blame, as I was just strapped into my seat and there was nothing I could have done differently. My main struggles are really the constant terror, being on edge, and feeling completely numb inside.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're observing yourself or the world around you from outside your body more frequently now, or is it just limited to certain situations or times of the day?\n\nPatient: It is mostly limited to specific moments, particularly when I am alone in my office or lying in bed at night. During those times, I feel like I’m watching myself from across the room and the world looks flat and unreal. It feels as if I’m stuck in a bad movie of that flight and haven’t fully returned to my own body yet.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of detachment and observing yourself from outside your body interfering with your daily life and relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes, they are heavily impacting both my work and home life. When I feel like I'm watching myself from across the room, it's impossible to stay present in meetings, which has forced me to walk out of them early. It also spills over at home, making me feel so disconnected that I can't feel any joy or warmth when my wife shares good news about our kids.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of detachment and observing yourself from outside your body getting worse, staying the same, or improving slightly since the incident occurred?\n\nPatient: They have been staying pretty much the same, without any real improvement, since the incident six days ago. Every night in bed or when I'm alone in my office, I feel that exact same unsettling detachment. It hasn't gotten any better at all, and it is just exhausting.\n\nDoctor: On a scale of 1 to 10, how would you rate your overall level of distress or suffering since the near-crash incident, with 1 being the least distressing and 10 being the most distressing?\n\nPatient: I would easily rate my distress as a 10. It is completely overwhelming and has totally disrupted my ability to run my company or even feel like myself, leaving me constantly on edge and exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the near-crash incident was your fault in any way, or that you could have prevented it from happening?"

Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYes, since this happened, I feel like someone flipped a switch inside me and left me completely numb. When my wife shares good news about our kids or my company hits a target, I just sit there unable to feel any joy or warmth.
